## The Svapnohana's watch lines in the Narthang and Co ne prints: is the fourth-watch line «ཐུན་བཞི་ཟླ་བ་ཕྱེད་བར་དུ» present, and how does the third watch's line read — q833, q870, q864, q865, q836 (RD280)

## <a id="svo-p271h"></a>svo:p271h The watch verse on the Narthang manuscript of the Svapnohana, read again by eye (RD280)

*Read: scan, 2026-11-10.*

> Narthang manuscript, W2KG5015 vol. 28 (I2KG212312 img. 284, printed p. 282, line 3, 3000 px strip, letters about 36 px; the third row of the frame), read on crops at 2–3×:
> …བརྒྱད་པ་ལ། །ཐུན་གསུམ་ཟླ་བ་གསུམ་གྱི་བར། །ཐུན་བཞི་ཟླ་བ་ཕྱེད་བར་དུ། །མཐོང་བར་འགྱུར་བ་ཐེ་ཚོམ་མེད། །སྐྱ་རེངས་ཤར་བའི་དུས་ཉིད་ལ།

...in the eighth month. The third watch: within three months. The fourth watch: within half a month. It will be seen, without doubt. At the time the dawn rises...
The watch verse in the prints that carry it, read again by eye (RD280). Read: the Narthang manuscript's page (I2KG212312 img. 284, printed p. 282), 2–3× crops of the third row of the frame, every syllable of the verse; the Narthang one-bit copy (W22704, I3279 img. 286) at 3–4× (letters broken, the verse located and not read syllable by syllable); the Co ne (I1GS66059 img. 270, lines 3–4) at 3×; the Svapnekṣā not reread (RD234, RD236 and RD276 stand). Result: the Narthang manuscript has the four segments, the second watch's «ཐུན་གཉིས་ཟླ་བ་བརྒྱད་པ་ལ», the third watch's «ཐུན་གསུམ་ཟླ་བ་གསུམ་གྱི་བར» (the shad follows «བར» directly, no «དུ», as the Derge etext; the Peking print has «བར་དུ»), and «ཐུན་བཞི་ཟླ་བ་ཕྱེད་བར་དུ» with «དུ» present (moderate; the stack is clear, the vowel-less «དུ» small); «མཐོང་བར་འགྱུར་བ་ཐེ་ཚོམ་མེད» and then the dawn line follow. The first segment's «བརྒྱད་པ་ལ» stands in the Narthang manuscript with པ (the first syllable's stack is cut at the crop's left edge; པ moderate, not «བར»), as Peking reads it at 2× (RD242), against the Derge etext's «བརྒྱད་བར་ལ». The Co ne's worn dotted ink did not give a syllable by eye at 3× (only the stack «ཐུན་བཞི» in line 4 is plain); its reading stays RD276's, by machine vote. Count by print as now known: four watches in the Derge etext, the two Narthang scans (one print family), the gser bris ma and the Dpe bsdur ma text; the Co ne carries the line by machine reading; three watches in the Peking print alone. The third watch's line ends «བར» in the Narthang and the Derge etext and «བར་དུ» in the Peking.

### Open (RD280)

- Question: does the Co ne print's fourth-watch line end «པར་དུ» or «པར་བྱ»? The ink is too broken to settle by eye (RD276's machine runs differ).
- Question: is the first segment «བརྒྱད་པ་ལ» (Narthang, Peking) or «བརྒྱད་བར་ལ» (Derge etext) the older reading, and which does the Dpe bsdur ma's collation give? The notes' markers are lost in the OCR.

### Searched and not found (RD280)

- The Narthang one-bit copy W22704 (I3279 img. 286) at 3–4×: letters broken by thresholding, not readable syllable by syllable; the manuscript replica of the same page is the legible witness.
- The Co ne img. 270 lines 3–4 at 3×: dotted, partly missing ink; no syllable of the watch lines told apart by eye except the stack «ཐུན་བཞི».



## Padma rdo rje's book that quotes the whole Svapnohana (about pp. 17–26): the sentence that introduces the quotation, its named source and author, and any gloss of the watches — q847, q796, q798 (RD282)

*Read: etext search snippets, 2026-11-10 (RD282); nothing new reached, no scan (the book's etext volume VEIE0OPI409E1122_I1KG10372 is search-only, `etextchunks` 403).*

The sentence that introduces Padma rdo rje's quotation of the *Svapnohana*, and the lines after it, were not reached; no passage is added. What the snippets fix about where they lie (etext character offsets of the volume, from the search links):

- The last paragraph reached before the quotation is the account of the dream's length on p. 16 (hit at char. 18559, «dus tshod rmi lam»); its snippet ends «… rmi lam thengs nyis khri rmis [• 16 •] zer ro» and a search cannot run on across the end of that paragraph.
- The quotation's first line («rmi lam sna tshogs las byung ba / de yi brtag pa bshad par bya») is at char. 21346 and the snippet begins with it, nothing before it in the same paragraph: about 2800 characters (the rest of p. 16 after the account, p. 17 up to the quotation) lie between and were not reached.
- Strings that would appear if he printed the work's Sanskrit and Tibetan title or the homage before the quotation («rgya gar skad du … swab+nA u hA na», «bod skad du rmi lam brtag pa», «sangs rgyas la phyag 'tshal lo» followed by «rmi lam sna tshogs») give no hit in his book: in the parts reached he does not print the title line or the homage of the Tengyur text, and the quotation opens directly with the verse (the snippet carries noise tokens, «da dag dag pa nas dag», between the lines).
- The quotation's end is at char. 25516 («… rmi lam ngan pa zlog par 'gyur / [722/6] rmi lam brtag pa rdzogs so / [2]de'i [26 .]»): it spans about 4200 characters. After the end marker a new paragraph begins whose first words are not known.

Open

Question: Padma rdo rje quotes the whole *Svapnohana* on about pp. 17–26 of his book: what does the sentence that introduces it say about its source and author, and does he gloss the watches after it? (Unchanged from q847; the scan, on archive.org, is lent only.)

Searched and not found

- About 90 further exact-string searches on BDRC (2026-11-10), in EWTS, for the paragraph between the end of p. 16 (char. 18559) and the quotation (char. 21346) and for the paragraph after «rmi lam brtag pa rdzogs so … [26 .]»: forms of «rmi lam brtag pa» with a following particle or noun (zhes bya ba, 'di, 'i bstan bcos, 'i le'u, ltar, nas, gsungs, bshad), «bstan 'gyur las rmi lam», «rgyal ba'i bstan 'gyur», the translator and author names in the forms «bi b+hU ti tsan+dra» (b+hU / b+hu / bhU / bhu), «slob dpon», «lo tsA ba», «ngag gi dbang phyug», «ngag dbang gi dgongs pa», «de ltar …» and «thun …» openings: either more than ten hits on BDRC (the list shows ten, none of them his book) or none. No string reached a chunk of his book between chars. 18559 and 21346 or after 25516.
- The author's own name or the work's title is not found in the parts reached: Ngag dbang appears only in the quotation's closing line («'di ni ngag dbang dgongs pa ste», the text's own line, not his comment).

## The Svapnohana's doubtful words on the Narthang manuscript, syllable by syllable — q813, q814, q825, q799, q834 (RD289)

## <a id="svo-p271i"></a>svo:p271i The Svapnohana's doubtful words on the Narthang manuscript (RD289)

*Read: scan, 2026-11-10.*

> Narthang manuscript, W2KG5015 vol. 28, I2KG212312:
> 
> img. 284 (printed p. 282) line 5, read by eye at 4–5×:
> རྨི་ལམ་རྣམས་ན་བགྲོད་མིན་ལ་ཞུགས་ཤིས་པའོ། །
> 
> img. 286 (printed p. 284) line 2 (about x 1230–1530 and 2350–2870 of 3000), line 4 (x 900–1120):
> གང་ལ་ཡང་དག་བཅུ་ཡི་བར། །
> རྒོད་མ་ཁྱིམ་བྱ་ཀྲ་ཨུ[ཉྩི]། །ཐོབ་ནས་གང་གི་གཉིད་སད་ན། །དེ་ཡིས་དེ་དུས་སྙིང་[སྡུག]་འཐོབ། །
> རྟ་ལ་མོ་དང་ལྷོ་འཕྱེ་དང་། །
> 
> img. 287 (printed p. 285) line 2:
> ག་ཡ་ཏྲི་ནི་སྟོང་ཕྲག་བཟླས། །

Five places of the Svapnohana on the Narthang manuscript's frames, read by eye (printed pages 282 to 285 are images 284 to 287, not 283 to 285; bracketed syllables are not settled): "in dreams, [the clause] 'what is not walked', entering it is auspicious" (p. 282); "the ten ... " (the line with 'the ten', p. 284); "...having got [a krauñca, a house-bird], whoever wakes, then [grief or what is dear to the heart] is got" (p. 284); "a horse, [the la mo], the pot-belly (lto 'phye) and ..." (p. 284); "the Gāyatrī recited a thousand times" (p. 285).

The Narthang manuscript (I2KG212312, one scribe's hand, letters about 36 px, 8 lines to the frame) carries the Svapnohana on four frames: img. 284 (printed p. 282; line 2 the title, lines 3–5 the verse of the watches and the first images), img. 285 (p. 283; the flesh eaten to the black-clothed woman), img. 286 (p. 284; yellow-clothed woman to the fire declared fearful and the burning seat; 8 lines) and img. 287 (p. 285; lines 1–3 the last verses, the remedy and the colophon, the next work's title begins on line 4). The whole text was read by Yigdzin-1 on the whole frames (img. 284 and 285) and on 2×-enlarged line strips (img. 284, 286, 287; the whole-frame run on img. 286 returned only the colophon of the previous work), and the doubtful places then by eye at 4–8× on contrast-stretched crops. The strips of this clean manuscript hand did not hallucinate as the Co ne's did (RD276); the machine text follows the Derge etext throughout except in the places below. Nothing of the other frames was compared syllable by syllable with the Derge etext.

By eye on the Narthang manuscript:

- «བགྲོད་མིན» (q813): img. 284 line 5 reads «རྨི་ལམ་རྣམས་ན་བགྲོད་མིན་ལ་ཞུགས་ཤིས་པའོ»; the syllable before «མིན» is བགྲོད (བ, ག with ྲ and ོ, final ད), not «བཟློག»; the next syllable's head (མ or ཤ) is not told apart from ཤ of «ཤིས» two syllables on, read as «མིན» like the three other prints (moderate). So the clause stands as «བགྲོད་མིན» in the Derge etext, Peking, Narthang manuscript and, by Yigdzin-1, the Co ne (RD276 leaned the same way); its sense stays open (q813).
- «བཅུ་ཡི་བར» (q814): img. 286 line 2 reads «གང་ལ་ཡང་དག་བཅུ་ཡི་བར»: བཅུ with ུ, then ཡི with the vowel ི clear (6×), then བར; not «པི» (the Co ne machine runs' doubt). Whether the ten are days is not in the line (the text before it is «ལག་པ་གཡས་པ་བཟུང་བ་ན», when the right hand is seized by a white snake).
- «རྟ་ལ་མོ» (q814, q825): img. 286 line 4 reads «རྟ་ལ་མོ་དང་ལྷོ་འཕྱེ་དང་» with a plain ལ, no subjoined ླ, at 8× (medium-high), so the Narthang manuscript agrees with the Peking and the two other Narthang scans (four prints against the Derge etext's «བླ་མོ»; RD276's Co ne runs read «བླ་མོ» by a curved foot, unsettled). The word after «དང་» looks «ལྷོ» (ལྷ with ོ), not the etext's «ལྟོ»: the stack was not told apart at 8× from the stack of the other prints (Yigdzin-1 also read «ལྷོ»); a doubt, not a variant.
- «དེ་ཡིས་དེ་དུས་སྙིང་སྡུག་འཐོབ» (q799): img. 286 line 2 end reads «དེ་ཡིས་དེ་དུས་སྙིང་[སྡུག]་འཐོབ»: «དེ་ཡིས» twice and «དུས» clear; the second syllable of «སྙིང་…» is a stack with ུ and final ག whose head was not told apart (སྡུག as the Derge etext; Yigdzin-1 reads «སྔག», not supported by the stack's shape at 7×), so the sense (what is dear to the heart, or sorrow) stays open: the Narthang gives no new word.
- The line before: «རྒོད་མ་ཁྱིམ་བྱ་ཀྲ་ཨུ[ཉྩི]། ཐོབ་ནས་གང་གི་གཉིད་སད་ན།» (img. 286 line 2, 4×): «རྒོད་མ་ཁྱིམ་བྱ» and «ཀྲ» read, the last word's final stack not told apart from «ཉྩི»/«ཙི» (Yigdzin-1 «ཨུན་ཙི»; Peking «ཨུཉྩི», moderate).
- The Gāyatrī (img. 287 line 2): «ག་ཡ་ཏྲི་ནི་སྟོང་ཕྲག་བཟླས» at 6×: three syllables ག, ཡ and a stack ཏྲ with a small vowel sign above (ི, not told apart from ེ; Yigdzin-1 «ཏྲེ»), then «ནི», «སྟོང་ཕྲག», «བཟླས»: a third syllable is there, which the Co ne's two-syllable «གས་ཏྲི» (RD276) lacks; spelling as in the Peking (svo-gayatri-spelling).
- The Sanskrit title (q834; img. 284 line 2, about x 1560–2300, 5–6×): after «རྒྱ་གར་» the signs run (from the right) a first stack that looks «སྭ» (hooked ཝ below ས), then a stack with a ba-like body, a stack «ན» or «ཎ», a «ཨུ», a «ཧ» and a last stack with ཱ; Yigdzin-1 on the strip reads «སྭབྣ་ཨུ་ཧཱ་ནཱ» (the frame's start reads «སྭ་བྣ་ཨུ་ཧཱ་ནཱ»). The eye agrees on the shapes of the first, fourth and fifth signs only; the second to third (བྣ/བྷྣ/པྣ) is not settled, so the title stays unsettled. Not «སྭཔྣ»: the Sanskrit svapna would need a pa-sign, and none is plain.

Count by print, as now known, of the places read by eye: «བགྲོད་མིན» in the Derge etext, Peking, Narthang manuscript (and the Dpe bsdur ma etext); «བཅུ་ཡི་བར» in the Derge etext, Peking, Narthang manuscript; «རྟ་ལ་མོ» with a plain ལ in the Peking and the three Narthang prints, «རྟ་བླ་མོ» in the Derge etext and the Dpe bsdur ma etext; the Gāyatrī of three syllables in the Peking, Narthang manuscript, Derge etext.

### Open (RD289)

- Question: is the third syllable of the Gāyatrī's first word «ཏྲི» (Peking) or «ཏྲེ» (Yigdzin-1 on the Narthang strip): the vowel sign above the stack is a small curve, ི and ེ are not told apart on it; and is the word the Sanskrit gāyatrī rendered «ག་ཡ་ཏྲི»?
- Question: the Sanskrit title of the Svapnohana: «སྭ…བྣ/པྣ…ཨུ་ཧཱ་ནཱ», which stack stands second and third on the Narthang manuscript (img. 284 line 2) and in the Peking print, and is the Sanskrit svapnoha or svapnoddhāra?

### Searched and not found (RD289)

- The Narthang manuscript's second and third title signs at 5–6× (img. 284 line 2): the stacks are ink-filled and not told apart; not settled.
- The Narthang manuscript's «ལྷོ»/«ལྟོ» (img. 286 line 4) and «སྙིང་སྡུག»/«སྔག» (line 2 end): the head of the stack is not told apart at 7–8×.
- The whole-frame run of Yigdzin-1 on img. 286 returns only 20 characters (a colophon); the frame needs line strips, as the Co ne frames do.

## <a id="svo-p271j"></a>svo:p271j The colophons of the Svapnohana and of the work before it, on the Peking print and the Narthang manuscript (RD292)

*Read: scan, 2026-11-10.*

> Peking, I1KG13197 img. 314 line 8 (end of the preceding work):
> རྒྱ་གར་གྱི་མཁན་པོ་དཱི་པཾ་ཀ་ར་ཤྲཱི་ཛྙཱ་ན་དང་། ལོ་ཙཱ་བ་དགེ་སློང་རིན་ཆེན་བཟང་པོས་བསྒྱུར་ཅིང་ཞུས་པའོ། (end of the line unread)
> 
> Peking, img. 318 line 6 (end of the Svapnohana):
> རྨི་ལམ་བརྟག་པ་རྫོགས་སོ། །རྒྱ་གར་ཤར་ཕྱོགས་ཀྱི་པཎྜི་ཏ་བི་བྷཱུ་ཏི་ཙནྡྲས་རང་འགྱུར་དུ་མཛད་པའོ། །
> 
> Narthang manuscript, I2KG212312 img. 287 line 3 (printed p. 285):
> རྨི་ལམ་བརྟག་པ་རྫོགས་སོ། །རྒྱ་གར་ཤར་ཕྱོགས་ཀྱི་པཎྜི་ཏ་བི་བྷཱུ་ཏི་ཙནྡྲས་རང་འགྱུར་དུ་མཛད་པའོ། །
> 
> BDRC work records (catalogue transcription, Wylie): MW23703_1748 'chi ba bslu ba'i man ngag: slob dpon ngag gi dbang phyug grags pas mdzad pa'i 'chi ba bslu ba bstan pa las le'u bzhi pa'o// ngag gi dbang phyug grags pas mdzad pa 'chi ba bslu ba zhes bya ba rdzogs so// rgya gar gyi mkhan po dI pam ka ra shrI dz+nyA na dang/ lo tsA ba dge slong rin chen bzang pos bsgyur ba'o//
> MW23703_1749: rmi lam brtag pa rdzogs so// rgya gar shar phyogs kyi paN+Di ta bi bhU ti tsandras rang 'gyur du mdzad pa'o//

Peking img. 314 line 8: "translated and revised by the Indian abbot Dīpaṃkaraśrījñāna and the lotsāba, the monk Rin chen bzang po" (the colophon of the work that stands before the Svapnohana; the words «བསྒྱུར་ཅིང་ཞུས» at 1.5×, the sense moderate). A short title-like tag follows at the end of the line, «རྨི་ལམ་བརྟག་པ་…བཞུགས་སོ» after a mark «༡༡», not told apart.

Peking img. 318 line 6: the end of the Svapnohana and its colophon, in the Derge wording: "made by the eastern Indian paṇḍita Vibhūticandra as his own translation"; the next line carries the title of the next work (a Padma-net Avalokiteśvara practice, «པདྨ་དྲྭ…སྤྱན་རས་གཟིགས…»), then the mark «༡༡» and the Sanskrit title line of that work.

Narthang manuscript img. 287 line 3: the same two sentences as the Derge text, «རང་འགྱུར་དུ་མཛད་པའོ» read plainly.

The BDRC transcription of the preceding work's colophon says: "Chapter four of the Teaching on Deceiving Death, made by the teacher Ngag gi dbang phyug grags pa; the Deceiving Death by Ngag gi dbang phyug grags pa is complete; translated by the Indian abbot Dīpaṃkaraśrījñāna and the lotsāba, the monk Rin chen bzang po." The Svapnohana's BDRC colophon is the Derge text's.

### Open (RD292)

- Question: is the «ངག་དབང» of «འདི་ནི་ངག་དབང་དགོངས་པ་སྟེ» the abbreviated name of Ngag gi dbang phyug grags pa (Vāgīśvarakīrti), whose Deceiving Death stands before the Svapnohana in the Derge, Peking and Narthang volumes, or another teacher, or a name of Mañjuśrī? (q796 unchanged: the colophons and BDRC's person records name nobody else; claims/svo-ngag-dbang-bdrc-records.md.)
- Question: how are the watches (ཐུན) bounded in the Svapnohana: four equal parts of the night, or otherwise? (q798 unchanged; claims/svo-watch-bounds-not-in-colophons.md.)

### Searched and not found (RD292)

- The colophons on the Derge, Peking and Narthang: no author named for the Svapnohana, no reviser, no place; the Peking line at the end of img. 314 line 8 after the preceding work's colophon (a title-like tag after the mark «༡༡») not told apart at 1.5×.
- The Derge dkar chag, the Peking and Narthang catalogues were not opened for this reading (no etext or scan located); the catalogue statements stand as RD257 recorded them.
- BDRC person records of both men: no work, teacher or pupil link between Vibhūticandra and Vāgīśvarakīrti; no entry names the remedy's Ngag dbang.

## <a id="svo-p271k"></a>svo:p271k The Svapnohana's three doubtful places on the Peking print again (RD305)

*Read: scan, 2026-11-10.*

> Peking, W1KG13126 vol. 28, I1KG13197:
> 
> img. 315 line 5 (x about 2750–3550, 4×):
> རྨི་ལམ་རྣམས་ན་བགྲོད་མིན་ལ་ཞུགས་ཤིས་པའོ། །
> 
> img. 317 line 3 (x about 1300–2700, 2.5×):
> གང་ལ་ཡང་དག་བཅུ་ཡི་བར། །དེ་ཡིས་
> 
> img. 317 line 5 (x about 2700–3450, 5×):
> གཞན་རྣམས་དཀར་པོ་མངོན་པར་དགའ། །རྟ་ལ་མོ་

Three places of the Svapnohana on the Peking print, read again by eye: "in dreams, [the clause] 'not walked', entering it is auspicious" (img. 315 line 5); "whoever ... the ten ..., (from) it wealth is certainly got" (img. 317 line 3); "the others, white, are delightful. A horse, la mo ..." (img. 317 line 5, the word «ལ་མོ» with a plain ལ). Other places of these lines are in svo:p271d.

RD305 read these on the Peking frames at 2.5–5× with the Derge text beside them. «བགྲོད་མིན»: the glyphs run ན, བ+ག with ྲ and ོ, then ད, མི, ན, ལ, ཞུགས, ཤིས, པ: the same clause as the Derge etext and the Narthang manuscript; the syllable before «མིན» is not «བཟློག» (moderate; the print is a blur of a few pixels per stroke). «བཅུ་ཡི་བར»: བཅུ, ཡི, བར clear at 2.5×, followed by «དེ་ཡིས»: no variant. «རྟ་ལ་མོ»: at 5× the word after «དགའ། །» is རྟ, then a ལ with its wedge and no subjoined ླ under the head, then མོ (high): the Peking carries a plain ལ. The next word «ལྟོ»/«ལྷོ» was not in the crops. The Derge print of the Tengyur is image group I1344 (img. 268–271), not I00JW501163, which is the Derge print of the Four Tantras and has no Svapnohana; the Derge Tengyur frame's letters are 8 px and were not read by eye again.

What «བགྲོད་མིན» limits (q813): the clause stands at the end of a list of ill signs (the entrails, the sword, the boat, blood flowing from the body) and before the next verse's «the king, the elephant, the best horse» in the Peking line 5 itself, with no particle that ties it to the line before or after except «ན» and «ལ་ཞུགས»; the reading «what is not walked is entered» against «dreams ... walking no more» is not decided by the print, so q813's sense stays open. The ten of «བཅུ་ཡི་བར» is not marked as days in the line either (it follows «when the right hand is seized by a white snake»).

### Open (RD305)

- Question: «བགྲོད་མིན་ལ་ཞུགས་ཤིས་པའོ» closes a list of ill signs; does it say that entering a path not to be walked is auspicious, or that the signs just listed are auspicious when one is not walking, and is there a commentary on the Svapnohana that glosses it?

### Searched and not found (RD305)

- The Derge print of the Svapnohana in the image group I00JW501163 named by the row: that group is the Derge Four Tantras (ch. 91 and the Root Tantra were searched on it by earlier readings); the Derge Tengyur frames are in I1344 img. 268–271, 8 px letters.
- A syllable of «བགྲོད་མིན» and of «བཅུ་ཡི་བར» that differs on the Peking: none seen at 2.5–4×.

## The Svapnohana on the Derge Tengyur frames (I1344 img. 268–271, letters of about 8 px, so only enlarged and contrast-stretched crops): «བགྲོད་མིན», «བཅུ་ཡི་བར», «བླ་མོ» and the word after it, on the Derge, to set beside the Peking and Narthang of RD305 and RD289 (q813, q814), and the Co ne print of the same (RD310)

## <a id="svo-p271l"></a>svo:p271l The three doubtful places of the Svapnohana on the Derge print, read by eye (RD310)

*Read: scan, 2026-11-10.*

> Derge Tengyur, I1344 (BDRC), the Svapnohana (the examination of dreams, རྨི་ལམ་བརྟག་པ):
> 
> img. 268 line 7 (x about 770–1130 of 1990, 10×):
> རྨི་ལམ་རྣམས་ན་བགྲོད་མིན་ལ་ཞུགས་ཤིས་པའོ། །
> 
> img. 270 line 3 (x about 1430–1700 of 2027, 12×):
> གང་ལ་ཡང་དག་བཅུ་ཡི་བར། །དེ་ཡིས་
> 
> img. 270 line 5 (x about 1085–1260 of 2027, 11–20×):
> གཞན་རྣམས་དཀར་པོ་མངོན་པར་དགའ། །རྟ་བླ་མོ་དང་ལྟོ་འཕྱེ་དང་།

Three places of the Svapnohana on the Derge print of the Tengyur, read by eye on crops of 10–20 times and with Yigdzin-1 on whole-line crops as a second witness. The Derge frames are small woodblock scans (1990–2050 by 310–330 px for seven lines), the letters 8 px: every reading is moderate at best.

"In dreams, [the clause] 'not walked', entering it is auspicious" (img. 268 line 7); "whoever ... the ten ..., (from) it wealth is certainly got" (img. 270 line 3); "the others, white, are delightful. A horse, bla mo, and the belly-walker" (img. 270 line 5).

Lines are numbered on the ink rows of the page: img. 268 has the colophon of the preceding work on lines 1–3, the Svapnohana title on line 4, and the clause on line 7; img. 270 line 3 holds «ལག་པ་གཡས་པ་བཟུང་བ་ན། །གང་ལ་ཡང་དག་བཅུ་ཡི་བར», line 5 the word after «དགའ། །».

«བགྲོད་མིན» (img. 268 line 7): after «རྨི་ལམ་རྣམས་ན» the stack with ྲ and ོ is བགྲོད (ག with the subjoined ྲ, a ོ over it, final ད), then མིན (མི with its vowel, ན), then ལ, ཞུགས, ཤིས (moderate). Not «བཟློག». Yigdzin-1 on the line crop reads «བགྲོད་མིན་ལ་ཞུགས་ཤིས་པའོ». «བཅུ་ཡི་བར» (img. 270 line 3): བཅུ with the round ུ foot, ཡི with a small vowel over it, བར, then «དེ་ཡིས»: no variant (moderate-high); Yigdzin-1 reads the same. «བླ་མོ» (img. 270 line 5): after «རྟ» the stack has two small loops one above the other (the ཝ-like head and the lower loop), where a plain ལ elsewhere on the same page (img. 270 line 3 «གང་ལ») has one loop and its wedge; so the Derge reads «བླ་མོ» with the subjoined ླ, as the etext (moderate-low; the loops are 2–3 px). Yigdzin-1 reads «རྟ་བླ་མོ་དང་ལྟོ་འཕྱེ་དང་». The word after «དང་»: a ལ ring over a hook (ྟ) reads «ལྟོ»; ྷ against ྟ not told apart at 18× (doubt, not a variant).

### Open (RD310)

- Question: on the Derge print the word before «ལྟོ་འཕྱེ» (the belly-walker, the snake) reads «བླ་མོ» by the two loops of its stack, against the plain ལ of the Peking and the Narthang prints; is the subjoined ླ a real variant of the Derge tradition (the Derge and the Dpe bsdur ma etext on one side, the Peking and Narthang on the other), and does a reader of the tradition know the word as «རྟ་བླ་མོ» (the centipede) in a list of creatures?

### Searched and not found (RD310)

- The Co ne print of the same three places by eye: the Co ne scans (RD277) were read by machine only and no new Co ne image was taken here; the places stay machine readings there.
- A variant of «བགྲོད་མིན» or «བཅུ་ཡི་བར» on the Derge frames at 10–12×: none seen; «བཟློག» for «བགྲོད» and «པི» for «ཡི» are not what the strokes show.
- A telling of ྷ from ྟ in the stack after «དང་» before «འཕྱེ» at 18×: not possible at 8 px letters.

## The Sanskrit dream texts beside the Svapnohana, from open editions and scans: the Bṛhadyātrā 16.10 (which part of the night and which humour go with which of the three seasons, q855), the Aṅgavijjā chs. 41–42 (the bright beginning and root as forenoon or afternoon, q856, q892), the Bṛhaspati Svapnādhyāya in Negelein's manuscripts and the Penn, Srinagar and Ramtek copies (three fortnights or a month for the fourth watch, q868, q871, q872), and the Aṣṭāṅgahṛdaya Śārīrasthāna 6.63 «gosarge» or «gomuktra» (q891) (RD312)

*Read: scan, 2026-11-10 (RD312), on the page images of the Internet Archive items (full size, crops of 1.5–2×); the Hindi preface of the Aṅgavijjā and the other editions of the Hṛdaya in their text layers (machine). The Sanskrit passages are written into the corpus file of their own source; this section is the reading's account and its answers.*

The passages of this reading, each with its edition, page, leaf and apparatus: [asm:p179-180](../asm/asm.md#asm-p179-180) (Aśvamedhīyayātrā 17.11 with Bhaṭṭotpala), [agv:p185](../agv/agv.md#agv-p185), [agv:p190b](../agv/agv.md#agv-p190b), [agv:p210](../agv/agv.md#agv-p210) (Aṅgavijjā chs. 41, 42, 53 with the notes), [ahr:p436](../ahr/ahr.md#ahr-p436) (Aṣṭāṅgahṛdaya, Nirṇayasāgara 1939), [ahs:i259](../ahs/ahs.md#ahs-i259) (Aṣṭāṅgasaṅgraha), [rmk:i2b](../rmk/rmk.md#rmk-i2b) and [pzs:i102-103b](../pzs/pzs.md#pzs-i102-103b) (the Ramtek and Srinagar copies of the Bṛhaspati Svapnādhyāya), and Negelein's p. 15 (neg:p14-16, read again on the image of leaf n44).

### q855: the parts of the night and the seasons of the Bṛhadyātrā's verse

A second recension of the Bṛhadyātrā (the Aśvamedhīyayātrā, ch. 17 v. 11 = Pingree's 16.10) is printed with **Bhaṭṭotpala's commentary**, which says the pairs. Verse, as printed (p. 179): «जघन्यमध्यप्रथमे निशांशे प्रावृट्शरे माधवसंज्ञिते च । काले मरुत्पित्तकफप्रकोपः साधारणः स्यात्खलु सन्निपातः ॥ ११ ॥» The gloss (p. 180): the last third of the night is the aggravation of wind, the middle third of bile, the first third of phlegm; and "in the rains, wind; in autumn, bile; in the spring called Mādhava, phlegm", in the other seasons the humour accumulates; the dream is seen with the signs of the humour at its height in that hour and season. So the order by position (last part, rains, wind; middle, autumn, bile; first, spring, phlegm) is the commentator's, a text of the tenth century, and no longer only the reader's. Claim: bhattotpala-pairs-night-parts-seasons-humours.

### q856 and q892: the bright beginning of the Aṅgavijjā, forenoon or afternoon

The apparatus of Puṇyavijaya's print was read at the three lists (ch. 41 p. 185, ch. 42 p. 190, ch. 53 p. 210). **None carries a note on the word for the time of the bright beginning**; the only note near it, on p. 185 line 29 (note 10), is on «आदिमूलीयेसु»: all manuscripts but Haṃ and Ta read «°मूलेसु». The editor used seven manuscripts (Haṃ, Ta of saṃvat 1505, Saṃ, a Loṃbaḍī-pāḍā one, Mo, his own, Si; preface, Hindi). So the afternoon of ch. 41 is, by the editor's practice of noting differences, the reading of his manuscripts, not a slip that he corrected silently or noted; but the lists differ among themselves in any case (afternoon twice in ch. 41; forenoon and afternoon in chs. 42 and 53; midday in chs. 41 and 42, midnight in ch. 53 for the bright middle). Which wording is the original is not decided by the print; a collation at p. 185 line 28 is the decider. Claim: angavijja-ch41-afternoon-no-variant-in-apparatus.

### q868, q871, q872: the Bṛhaspati Svapnādhyāya, the third and fourth watch

- **Negelein's i** (Leipzig 1178; p. 15, leaf n44): «svapnasya prathame yāme phalaṃ saṃvatsaraṃ bhavet | dvitīye cā'ṣṭame māse triyāme ca tripakṣake || tūrye yāme tu yaḥ svapno māsena phalado bhavet | aruṇodaya-(pra)velāyāṃ daśāhena phalaṃ bhavet ||». There is **no note or mark** on «tripakṣake»; the verse is given as "like s 2–3" (the Matsya's text); the print does not say that the manuscript reads it so or that he amended it. The Leipzig manuscript was not found in any open scan.
- **Srinagar** (pzs:i102-103b, re-read on the images at full size): «द्वितीये चाष्टभिर्मासैस्त्रिभिर्मासैस्त्रियामके । चतुर्थे प्रहरे मासेन फलं चात्र न संशयः ॥»: third «triyāmake», fourth a month; clear.
- **Ramtek** (rmk:i2b, re-read at 2×, the first page on its large image): «द्वितीये चाष्टभिर्मासैस्तृतीये [त्रि]मासके । चतुर्थे यामे तु यः स्वप्नो मासेन फल[दः …]»: the third watch in a **locative in «-māsake»** (the conjunct before it looks like «tri»; moderate), the fourth a month, in the wording of Negelein's i «caturthe yāme tu yaḥ svapno māsena phala-» (his «tūrye yāme tu yaḥ svapno māsena phalado bhavet»). So the Ramtek text has the form that a «tripakṣake» corrupts best: «trimāsake» (three months) and «tripakṣake» differ in the second member only. It makes it likely, and does not prove, that i's «three fortnights» is a corruption or a re-reading of «trimāsake» (q872) and that the Bṛhaspati text's fourth watch was a month from the start (q871): every copy read (Penn 1871 and 673, Srinagar, Ramtek, Negelein's i, a, f, m, n, q) has a month, the half-month is the Brahmavaivarta's and the Svapnohana's alone; the Odia print's month and a half, glossed fifteen days, is a third thing. Which reading is older is not settled by any copy dated before the 18th century (none is). Claim: neg-i-tripaksake-unmarked-ramtek-masake.
- **Penn 1871** (the leaf with the verse, 3014_0002): the OPenn server delivered the image at about 0.2–1 kB/s; the verse lines were read by RD277 on a part of the image and are not read anew here (see the end of this section).

### q891: «gosarge» or «gomārge» in the Aṣṭāṅgahṛdaya

Śārīrasthāna 6.63 in the **Nirṇayasāgara edition of 1939** (p. 436, with variant readings of the manuscripts under the text): «दृष्टः करोति तुच्छं च गोसर्गे तदहर्महत्» with no variant mark on «gosarge»; the marks of the page (11–17) lie on «ca» (अ: «vā»), on «nidrayā vā 'nupahataḥ» («cānu» in four manuscripts) and on commentary words. The commentary there glosses «gavāṃ mokṣakāle». The text layers of Vidyāsāgara 1860, Navare–Śarmā, Sengupta, the Kashi series and Atrideva Gupta's edition read the same (machine); none of them reads «gomārge» or «gomarge». The elder Vāgbhaṭa's Aṣṭāṅgasaṅgraha (ahs:i259) has «गोसर्गे महत्सद्यश्च», "at the cows' release great and at once", the Hṛdaya's same-day fruit made immediate. The Śabdakalpadruma's «gomarge» is the dictionary's type (ma and sa alike), not a variant of the manuscripts. The older manuscripts themselves were not seen. Claim: ahr-gosarge-no-gomarge-in-six-prints.

### Open (RD312)

- Question: in Puṇyavijaya's ch. 41 (p. 185, line 28), do the manuscripts Haṃ and Ta, the oldest of the seven, read «avaraṇhesu» for the time of the bright beginning, as the print has it, and do the others read «puvvaṇhesu» without the editor's noting it (the editor's note 10 at the same line concerns «ādimūlesu»)?
- Question: does the Leipzig manuscript 1178 read «tripakṣake», «trimāsake» or another word for the third watch, and the Ramtek M 856 «trimāsake»?
- Question: do the older manuscripts of the Aṣṭāṅgahṛdaya (the Nirṇayasāgara edition's अ इ ट ड ण त स म, Kunte's) all read «gosarge» at 6.63, and is the Aṣṭāṅgasaṅgraha's «sadyaḥ» (at once) against the Hṛdaya's «tadahaḥ» (the same day) a difference of the two works or of their copies?

### Searched and not found (RD312)

- Penn Ms. Coll. 390 Item 2730's first leaf (the span verse) and Item 1871's later leaves and colophon, Item 673's verse lines: the OPenn server (openn.library.upenn.edu) delivers an image of 1.6 MB at 0.2–1 kB/s, so the leaves arrived as their first lines only and were not read through in this session's time.
- The Leipzig manuscripts 1176–1180 (Negelein's f, i, m, n): no scan, no catalogue text open found.
- Hemādri's Āyurvedarasāyana in the two Gurukul Kangri scans of the Internet Archive (partly damaged, missing pages): no gloss on Śārīra 6.63 found in the text layers.
- A commentary on the Bṛhadyātrā dream chapter other than Bhaṭṭotpala's: none found; the *Yogayātrā* has no dream chapter (RD263).
- The Sanskrit text of the Aṣṭāṅgahṛdaya's Śārīrasthāna on GRETIL: not there (RD295); the prints above were read instead.

## The Co ne prints of the Svapnohana (vol. 28, 133b3–135a3, img. 270–273) and the Svapnekṣā (vol. 49, 130a1–130b7): does the Co ne read «བགྲོད་མིན» or «བཟློག་མིན», «འགྲོན་བུ» or the other word (q864), «ལ་མོ»/«བླ་མོ» and the watch lines (q865), the fourth-watch line «ཐུན་བཞི་ཟླ་བ་ཕྱེད་བར་དུ» (q870) and its ending «པར་དུ» or «པར་བྱ» (q873), and the second-watch line's first segment «བརྒྱད་པ་ལ» or «བརྒྱད་བར་ལ» (q874) (RD311)

The Co ne print of the Svapnohana (the examination of dreams, རྨི་ལམ་བརྟག་པ), read again by Yigdzin-1 on deskewed line strips of the bitonal frames img. 270, 272 and 273 (vol. 28, 133b-135a): "the second watch: within the eighth month ... the second (third) watch: within three ... the fourth watch: within half a month ... seen, without doubt"; "in dreams, [the clause] 'not walked'..., entering it is auspicious"; "a king, an elephant, the best horse; a guest, oxen and cows"; "whoever ... the ten ..., (from) it wealth is certainly got"; "the others, white, are delightful. A horse, bla mo, the belly-walker"; "the Gayatri recited a thousand times: by it peace is got".

The tallies are in the claims; the doubtful syllables are machine votes, not eye readings.

Method. The frames img. 270, 272, 273 (2055 × 486 and 2030 × 492 px, bitonal) were deskewed (−0.75°, −0.25°, −0.5°), cut by the ink rows into lines (img. 270 lines at y 135, 178, 217, 252, 289, 326, 359, 402), and Yigdzin-1 was run through the slot on 168 half-line strips (2×, 2.5×, 3×, blur 0–0.8), 172 windows of 330–350 px and 132 longer strips of one line (x from the start or from 650–1450 to the end, 1.5–3×, blur 0–0.8). Narrow windows of a few syllables return nothing or invented text, and the right half of a line as a strip returns invented text («འབུམ་ཁ་པ་བཞུགས་སོ»): the model needs the line's own run, and answers best at 1.5× with a blur of 0.5–0.8; a reading counts only where its words are the print's. By eye (4–6× crops, blur 0.5) the dots of the ink did not give one letter of the watch lines, of «བགྲོད/བཟློག», of the word after «བཅུ» or of the Gāyatrī's first word; so nothing here is a scan reading, and every vote below is of the machine.

Tallies of the runs that gave the place (RD276's and RD273's runs, in svo:p271g, counted with these):

- Watch lines (img. 270 line 4): the second watch's first segment ends «བརྒྱད་པར» with ར in every run that reached it (nine here, and RD276's «བརྒྱད་པར་ལ»); never «བརྒྱད་པ་ལ» and never «བརྒྱད་བར་ལ»; the syllable after it is «ལ» (3), «བ» (4), «བྱ» (2), «ཐ» (2): not settled. The third watch's line is «ཐུན་གཉིས་གསུམ་གྱི་བར» (five runs here, and RD276's) — the shorter form, without «ཟླ་བ», with «གཉིས» where the Narthang prints have «ཐུན་གསུམ»; one 2×–3× run reads «ཐུན་གསུམ་གསུམ་གྱི་བར». The fourth watch's line: «ཐུན་བཞི» (the 1.5× blur 0.8 run «ཐུན་གཞི», RD276's «ཐུན་བཞི»), «ཟླ་བ» present in that run and in RD276's two, absent in RD273's, «ཕྱེད»; the ending «པར» + «ལ» (two runs here), «པ» (one), «དུ» (RD276's two and RD273's), «བྱ» (RD276's two): so «པར», never «བར», and the last syllable not settled between «དུ» and «བྱ», the new runs adding «ལ».
- «བགྲོད་མིན»/«བཟློག་མིན»: one new run reaches the end of line 6 (1.5× blur 0.8) and reads «རྨི་ལམ་རྣམས་ན་བཟློག», as RD273's crop; RD276's two read «བགྲོད»; the line 7 start reads «མིན་ལ་ཞུགས་ཤིས་པའོ» in the 1.5× runs (6) and «མིན་མ་ཞུགས» in the others. Tally with RD273 and RD276: «བཟློག» 2, «བགྲོད» 2, undecided; the scribe's own «བགྲོད» elsewhere (img. 271) stays the only support for «བགྲོད».
- «འགྲོན་བུ»/«འགྲོན་དུ» (line 7): the runs on the line from its start to x 1300 read «འགྲོན་དུ» in 11 of 12, the shorter ones «བུ» in 5 (with «འགྲོད»/«འགྲོན»); with RD276's (3 : 2 for «དུ») the vote is «འགྲོན་དུ» about 15 to 7; moderate-low.
- «བཅུ་ཡི་བར» (img. 272 line 3): «བཅུའི་བར» in two windows here and in RD276's three, «བཅུ་པ་བར»/«བཅུ་བ་པར»/«བཅུ་བ་བར» in four, «བཅུ་པི་བར» in RD273's and one, «བསྐུལ་བར» in two (the 3× runs, worthless): a vowel sign after «བཅུ» is not decided, and «ཡི» is neither confirmed nor excluded.
- «བླ་མོ» (img. 272 line 4, end): «རྟ་བླ་མོ» in 14 of 16 runs here and in all of RD276's; «ཉམ་མོ» in two 3× runs; no run reads a plain «ལ་མོ»; the word after it «ལྟོ་འཕྱེད» (not «ལྷོ»; «འཕྱེད» for the Derge's «འཕྱེ»).
- The Gāyatrī (img. 273 line 1): «གས་ཏྲི» in all nine runs that reached it (RD276's, RD273's and eight here): two syllables, never «ག་ཡ་ཏྲི».

### Open (RD311)

- Question: in the Co ne print the second watch's first segment machine-reads «བརྒྱད་པར་» with a ར (not «བརྒྱད་པ་ལ» as the Narthang and Peking, not «བརྒྱད་བར་ལ» as the Derge etext), and the fourth watch's «ཕྱེད་པར་» likewise with པ before ར; is the Co ne's wording «པར» a variant of the Co ne tradition, and does the Dpe bsdur ma note it?
- Question: the Co ne's third watch line reads «ཐུན་གཉིས་གསུམ་གྱི་བར» (no «ཟླ་བ», «གཉིས» for «གསུམ»); is it the scribe's slip or the print's own text?

### Searched and not found (RD311)

- The Co ne's watch lines, «བགྲོད/བཟློག», the word after «བཅུ» and the Gāyatrī's first word by eye on deskewed, blurred crops at 4–7×: the ink is dotted and partly absent; not one letter told.
- The Svapnekṣā (vol. 49 img. 263–264, q865): left half-lines of img. 263 were read by Yigdzin-1 (the opening lines, the three parts of the night of svi:p130, the images); the Svapnekṣā has no watch lines and no «ལ་མོ» (svi:p130, q865's own note); the right halves of the strips returned nothing or invented text and no further reading was made; the Co ne's Svapnekṣā «དང་པོའི་ཆ…གཉིས་པར…ཐ་མར» line reads as in svi:p130.
- Narrow windows (330–350 px) of the line and the right half of a line as a strip: nothing usable; whole-line strips at 1.5× give the stretches above.

Nothing read yet: RD311's passages, with the English beside them, go here.

## <a id="svo-p271n"></a>svo:p271n Mipham's notes and WB on the night's halves: sunset, midnight, dawn (RD314)

*Read: etext, 2026-11-10.*

> WB vol. 1 p. 375 (the sky dog's facing by season, WB's own verse; 1996 etext VEIE0OPI51524892_I1KG12906, etext p. 385 = printed p. 375):
> 
> དཔྱིད་གསུམ་ཉི་ནུབ་ནམ་ཕྱེད་བར། །ཁ་ནི་ལྷོ་བལྟ་ནམ་ཕྱེད་ནས། །ཉི་ཤར་བར་ལ་ཁ་ནུབ་བལྟ། །ཉི་ཤར་ཉི་ཕྱེད་བར་བྱང་བལྟ། །ཉི་ཕྱེད་ཉི་ནུབ་བར་དུ་ཤར། །དབྱར་གསུམ་ཉི་གོང་ནམ་ཕྱེད་བར། །ཁ་ནུབ་ཕྱེད་ནས་ནམ་ལངས་བར། །ཁ་ནི་བྱུང་བལྟ་ནམ་ལངས་ནས། །ཉི་ཕྱེད་བར་དུ་ཤར་དུ་བལྟ། །ཕྱེད་ནས་ཉི་ནུབ་བར་དུ་ལྷོ། །སྟོན་གསུམ་ཉི་ནུབ་ནམ་ཕྱེད་བར། །ཁ་བྱང་ཕྱེད་ནས་ཉི་ཤར་བར། །ཤར་བལྟ་ཤར་ནས་ཉི་ཕྱེད་ལྷོ། །ཉི་ཕྱེད་དགོངས་པར་ནུབ་ཏུ་བལྟ། །དགུན་གསུམ་ཉི་མ་ནུབ་པ་དང་། །ནམ་ཕྱེད་བར་ལ་ཤར་དུ་བལྟ། །ནམ་ཕྱེད་ཉི་ཤར་བར་ལ་ལྷོ། །ཤར་ནས་ཕྱེད་བར་ནུབ་ཏུ་བལྟ།
> 
> Mipham's notes on WB (vol. 2 p. 532, etext p. 540), on the same rule:
> 
> དུས་བཞིའི་ཁ་ལྟ་ཕྱོགས་དང༌། ས་བདག་ལེའུ་ལྟར་བརྩིས་པས་སྟོན་གསུམ་ཉི་ནུབ་ནས་ནམ་ཕྱེད་བར་བྱང་ལ་བལྟ་དུས། ལི་ལ་ཁ་ཟོན་ལ་མཆེ་གཡས་ཁོན་ལ་མཆེ་གཡོན་ཡོད་པས་བྱང་ནས་ལྷོར་སོང་ན་ཁ། ནུབ་བྱང་ནས་ཤར་ལྷོར་སོང་ན་མཆེ་གཡས། བྱང་ཤར་ནས་ལྷོ་ནུབ་སོང་ན་མཆེ་གཡོན་ལ་ཕྲད་པ་དང་། ལྷོ་ནས་བྱང་དུ་སོང་ན་ལྟོ་བ་དང་ཕྲད་པ་ལྟ་བུའོ།
> 
> The same notes, the one place where they name a watch-like word of the day-and-night (vol. 2 p. 542, etext p. 550, in a list of the hand reckoning's and the wheel's words): «ཐུན་མཚམས་རཱ་ཧུ། མིག་དམར་རཱ་ཧུ། … རྣལ་འབྱོར་མ། བིཏྟི། ཐུན་ཕྱེད་སོགས། དུས་མཚན་མ།»

The quotations are the 1996 etext, not read on the scan in this reading (the verse of p. 375 was read on the scan in earlier readings, see wb.1:p375).

WB's verse, in the sky dog's reckoning, gives the direction the dog's mouth looks (ཁ་ལྟ, kha lta) in each season by four stretches of the day-and-night, always cut at the same events. Spring: sunset to midnight south; midnight to sunrise west; sunrise to noon north; noon to sunset east. Summer: (to) midnight west; midnight to dawn (ནམ་ལངས, nam langs) north (the etext prints «བྱུང»); dawn to noon east; noon to sunset south. Autumn: sunset to midnight north; midnight to sunrise east; sunrise to noon south; noon to dusk west. Winter: sunset and midnight east, midnight to sunrise south, sunrise to noon west, to the end north. The directions are as the etext prints them and are not re-checked here. The night is cut at midnight (ནམ་ཕྱེད, nam phyed) into two halves, the day at noon (ཉི་ཕྱེད, nyi phyed); no watch (ཐུན, thun) is named.

Mipham's note on it: counting by the earth-lord chapter, in the three autumn months, from sunset to midnight, while the dog looks north, the mouth and the right and left tusks lie so that, going from north to south one meets the mouth, from north-west to south-east the right tusk, from north-east to south-west the left tusk, and from south to north the belly. He repeats the chapter's halves of the night and adds nothing about watches.

The notes' only words of the thun kind outside this: the session Rāhu (ཐུན་མཚམས་རཱ་ཧུ, thun mtshams rahu) and the half-watch (ཐུན་ཕྱེད, thun phyed) as names in a list of what the hand reckoning and the wheel give; no bound of a watch is stated.

## <a id="svo-p271o"></a>svo:p271o The day-and-night in eight watches in the Hevajra and Kālacakra commentaries (RD314)

*Read: etext, 2026-11-10.*

> 1. Ratnākaraśānti (Rin chen 'byung gnas zhi ba), Muktāvalī on the Hevajra (དཔལ་དགྱེས་པའི་རྡོ་རྗེའི་དཀའ་འགྲེལ་མུ་ཏིག་ཕྲེང་བ), Derge Tengyur etext VE23703_1320 (MW23703_1189), etext p. 468; the same text in the Dpe bsdur ma etext VEIE0OPIA3E40644_I1PD95847 (MW1PD95844_0084), etext p. 1366:
> 
> འཕོ་བ་བཅུ་དྲུག་ཅེས་པ་ནི་སྣའི་བུ་ག་དེ་གཉིས་ཉིད་ནས་ཐུན་ཕྱེད་ཕྱེད་རྒྱུའོ། །ཉིན་མཚན་གྱི་འཕོ་བ་བཅུ་དྲུག་ཏུ་འགྱུར་རོ། །དབྱུ་གུ་དྲུག་ཅུ་རྩ་བཞི་ཞེས་པ་ནི་ཉིན་མཚན་གྱིས་སོ། །དབྱུ་གུ་གཉིས་ནི་ཆུ་ཚོད་དོ། །ཆུ་ཚོད་སུམ་ཅུ་རྩ་གཉིས་སུ་ཉིན་མཚན་ཉིད་ཀྱིས་སོ། །ཐུན་གྱི་བཞི་ཆ་ནི་ཆུ་ཚོད་དོ། །ཐུན་བཞི་ཞེས་པ་ནི་ཉིན་མོའམ་མཚན་གཅིག་ལ་སྟེ་ཉིན་ཞག་གཅིག་པ་ནི་བརྒྱད་དོ། །དེ་ལ་ཚིགས་སུ་བཅད་པ་ནི། མཚན་མོ་ཐུན་བཞི་ཉིན་མོ་སྟེ། །ཐུན་གཅིག་བཞི་ཆ་ཆུ་ཚོད་དོ། །
> 
> 2. Zhwa dmar IV Chos grags ye shes, a commentary on the Hevajra (དཔལ་ཀྱེའི་རྡོ་རྗེའི་རྒྱུད་ཀྱི་རྒྱལ་པོའི་སྙིང་པོ་གསལ་བར་བྱེད་པའི་སྒྲོན་མེ), etext VEIE0OPI22333339_I1KG4882, etext p. 681 (the sentence is in his gsung 'bum twice; the second copy, in a ritual for the arhats, MW1KG4876_676E94, has the same lines):
> 
> རྒྱས་པར་སློབ་དཔོན་བྷ་ཝ་བྷ་དྲས་འདིའི་འགྲེལ་པ་དང༌། མཁས་པ་རཏྣ་ཀ་ར་ཤཱནྟི་པས་བཤད་ཅིང་། ཕྱི་མས། ཉིན་མོ་མཚན་མོ་ཐུན་བཞི་སྟེ། །ཐུན་གཅིག་བཞི་ཆ་ཆུ་ཚོད་དོ། །དབྱུ་གུ་གཉིས་ལ་ཆུ་ཚོད་གཅིག །སྣ་བུག་གཉིས་ན་འགྲོ་འོང་རླུང་། །དབུགས་དྲུག་གིས་ནི་སྲོག་གཅིག་སྟེ། །སྲོག་དེ་ལྔ་བཅུ་ཐམ་པ་དང་། །དབུགས་ནི་གསུམ་དང་བཞི་ཆ་གསུམ། །བྱང་དང་ལྷོ་རུ་འགྲོ་བ་ཡི། །ཉིན་མཚན་དང་པོ་དག་ལའོ། །ཞེས་སོ།
> 
> 3. Padma dkar po, etext VE10736_015, etext p. 34:
> 
> འདི་དང་རྡོར་འཕྲེང་སོགས་སུ་ཉིན་ཞག་གཅིག་ལ་དབྱུག་གུ་དྲུག་ཅུ་རྩ་བཞི། དེ་གཉིས་བསྡོམས་པའི་ཆུ་ཚོད་སོ་གཉིས། དེ་གཉིས་བསྡོམས་པའི་འཕོ་བ་བཅུ་དྲུག དེ་གཉིས་བསྡོམས་པའི་ཐུན་ཚོད་བརྒྱད་དུ་གསུངས་པ་ནི་རྩའི་ཐོབ་སྐལ་བརྩིས་པ་ཡིན་ལ།
> 
> 4. Rang byung rdo rje, etext VE30541_013, etext p. 165:
> 
> འཁོར་ལོ་གཞན་ཐམས་ཅད་ནི་ཐུན་ཚོད་བརྒྱད་ཀྱི་རང་བཞིན་ཡིན་པས། ཐུན་ཚོད་བརྒྱད་ཀྱི་རྩ་བརྒྱད་བསྣན་པས་ན་སོ་དྲུག་ཏུ་བྱེད་པ་ནི། འགྲེལ་པ་ཉིད་དུ་གསལ་ཞིང༌།

> 5. Chos kyi grags pa, etext VE22082_012, etext p. 13:

> དེའང་ཞག་གཅིག་ལ་ཐུན་ཚོད་བརྒྱད། ཐུན་ཚོད་རེ་ལ་འཕོ་བ་གཉིས་རེ། འཕོ་བ་རེ་ལ་ས་ཆུ་མེ་རླུང་གི་དབྱུག་གུ་བཞི་རེ་ཡོད་པས་དྲུག་ཅུ་རྩ་བཞི་ཡིན་ལ།

> 6. A meditation instruction in the Rin chen gter mdzod chen mo (BDRC's label; the same words in Ngag dbang dpal bzang's gsung 'bum), etext VE1KG14_040, etext p. 36:
> 
> ཕྱིའི་ཐུན་ཚོད་ཉིན་མོ་ཡིན་ན་ཐུར་ཤིང་གི་གྲིབ་མ་ལྟ་བུས་ཉི་གྲིབ་གཞལ་ཏེ་གྲངས་བརྩི། མཚན་མོ་ལྟ་བུ་དང་ནང་གི་ཐུན་ཚོད་དབུགས་འགྱུ་བའི་གྲངས་ཀྱིས་གཟུང་སྟེ། ཐུན་ཚོད་རེ་ལ་དབུགས་ཕར་གཏོང་ཚུར་རྔུབ་ཀྱི་ཁུགས་པ་བདུན་སྟོང་ཉིས་བརྒྱ་རེ་འབྱུང་ཞིང༌། ཉིན་ཞག་ཕྲུགས་རེ་ལ་ཐུན་ཚོད་བརྒྱད་རེ་འབྱུང་བས་ནམ་ཟླ་དུས་བཞིའི་དབང་གིས་ཉིན་མཚན་རིང་ཐུང་ལ་དཔགས་ཏེ། ཉིན་བཞི་དང་མཚན་བཞིར་བགོས་ཏེ་ཆ་དབྱེ་ཞིང་ཚད་ལ་ཕབ་སྟེ་བསྒོམས་པས་ཁམས་མི་འཁྲུགས། …ཐུན་ཚོད་བརྒྱད་པོའང་གཉིས་གཉིས་སུ་བཅད་དེ་ཐུན་ཆུང་བཅུ་དྲུག་ཏུ་བགོས་པ་ཉིན་མོ་གྲིབ་ཚོད་ཀྱིས་གཞལ་ཏེ་གྲིབ་ཚོད་རེ་རེ་བསྒོམ།

All six are etext only (BDRC etexts, the Tibetan as the etext prints it; no scan opened; the page numbers are the etext's page markers). Read for the Hevajra and Kālacakra traditions' reckoning of the day-and-night in watches (ཐུན, thun; also ཐུན་ཚོད, thun tshod, "watch-time").

1. The Muktāvalī on the Hevajra, glossing a line about the breath: "Sixteen moves (འཕོ་བ, 'pho ba)" means that out of the two nostrils [the breath] goes in half-watches (ཐུན་ཕྱེད, thun phyed) each; the moves of the day-and-night become sixteen. "Sixty-four dbyu gu" is by the day-and-night. Two dbyu gu are one water-hour (ཆུ་ཚོད, chu tshod). Thirty-two water-hours are by the day-and-night itself. A quarter of a watch is a water-hour. "Four watches" is by day or by night, one night; a day-and-night is eight. On this the verse: "The night, four watches, [and] the day; one watch, its quarter, is a water-hour."

2. The fourth Zhwa dmar, a commentary on the Hevajra, gives the same verse (as the later of two: "in full the teacher Bhavabhadra's commentary and the learned Ratnākaraśānti have explained, and the latter [says]"): day and night four watches [each]; a watch's quarter is a water-hour; two dbyu gu one water-hour; the breath goes in and out at the two nostrils; six breaths are one life-breath (སྲོག, srog); and so on to the breaths north and south in the first day-and-night. The line after is a breath count (not needed here).

3. Padma dkar po (on the Kālacakra's and the Vajra Garland's breath reckoning): in the day-and-night 64 dbyug gu; the sum of two of them, 32 water-hours; of two of these, 16 moves; of two of these, eight watch-times (ཐུན་ཚོད, thun tshod): this is the reckoning of the channels' shares.

4. Rang byung rdo rje (the Kālacakra's channels): all the other wheels are of the nature of the eight watch-times; adding the eight channels of the eight watch-times makes thirty-six, as the commentary says.

5. Chos kyi grags pa: in one day-and-night eight watch-times; in each watch-time two moves; in each move four dbyug gu of earth, water, fire and wind; so sixty-four.

6. A meditation instruction (a hand-book of sessions): the outer watch-time by day is measured by the shadow of a post, the night's and the inner watch-times by the count of breaths; each watch-time has 7,200 breaths; a day-and-night has eight watch-times; "following the season's long or short day-and-night" (ནམ་ཟླ་དུས་བཞིའི་དབང་གིས་ཉིན་མཚན་རིང་ཐུང་ལ་དཔགས་ཏེ), it is divided into four of the day and four of the night; the eight are cut into two each as sixteen small watches (ཐུན་ཆུང་, thun chung), the day's measured by the shadow-hour.

(The text of 6 stands, with a gap marked «…», for the stretch about the effect of sitting; only the division is given.)

> བྱང་ཆུབ་ཤིང་དྲུང་དུ་གཤེགས་ནས། །རྡོ་རྗེའི་སྐྱིལ་ཀྲུང་ཐུན་གཅིག་གིས། །བདུད་དམག་སྲོད་ལ་ཚར་བཅད་དེ། ནམ་གྱི་ཆ་སྟོད་བསམ་གཏན་བཞི། །གུང་ལ་མངོན་ཤེས་ལྔ་བསྐྱེད་དེ། །རྡོ་རྗེའི་རྣལ་འབྱོར་བཞེས་པ་ནས། །ཐོ་རངས་རྒྱལ་རྔ་རྡུང་བའི་ཚེ། … བཅོ་ལྔའི་མཚན་གྱི་གུང་ཐུན་ལ། །རྩྭ་མཆོག་གྲོང་དུ་ཐུབ་པའི་དབང་།

All etext only (no scan opened in this reading; the pages are the etext's markers).

1. Tsong kha pa, on what a bodhisattva keeps in the preliminary conduct: knowing the measure of food, so as not to fall into the two extremes of too full and too hungry; "dividing the night into three watches (ཐུན་གསུམ, thun gsum), lying down in the middle one, and in the first and last [watch] and in the day doing recitation and yoga: not lying down in the night's upper part and lower part (ནམ་གྱི་ཆ་སྟོད་དང་ཆ་སྨད, nam gyi cha stod dang cha smad), but being devoted to yoga".

2. The Abhidharmasamuccaya's commentary quotes the verse "from the same [source]": "The wise one, all day and in the night's upper and lower watches, abides [in practice]; even in sleep not without fruit, mindful, lies until [waking]", and glosses it the same way as 1: the night divided in three watches, no sleep in the upper and lower, devotion to calm abiding and insight.

3. A Sakya Vinaya treatise, on the dawn: "When does the dawn arise? Some say: dividing the night into three watches, it arises in the middle of the last watch; dividing into nine, from the middle of the seventh; since the commentary on the bhikṣuṇī's prātimokṣa says 'in the middle of the last watch, when the king's drum is near'. This is not tenable, because it conflicts with your own words in giving the time ('the last watch, the middle of the last watch'; 'dawn not risen / dawn risen'). So, dividing the night into nine, it arises from the middle of the eighth: since the scripture says 'it arises when the eighth part is left' and 'in the first half of the last watch, when the king's drum is near'; and the ninth of nine parts is the last watch, [and] its first half is that eighth part." The last clause as the etext has it is not clear (the ninth part's first half cannot be the eighth part); it stays a doubt. Page numbers inside the text («160», «156») are footnote-like numbers of the etext.

4. WB, on the night of awakening (p. 22): seated under the bodhi tree in the vajra posture, "in one sitting" (ཐུན་གཅིག, thun gcig, one session) he defeated Māra's army at dusk (སྲོད, srod); in the first part of the night (ནམ་གྱི་ཆ་སྟོད) the four meditations; in the middle (གུང, gung) he produced the five higher cognitions; and when taking the vajra yoga, at dawn (ཐོ་རངས, tho rangs), when the royal drum is beaten, the full awakening. On p. 23, the Kālacakra was taught "in the middle watch (གུང་ཐུན, gung thun) of the night of the fifteenth" of the month.

### Open (RD314)

- Question: which author wrote the Sakya Vinaya treatise that sets the dawn (སྐྱ་རེངས) against the night's three watches and nine parts (svo:p271p, item 3), from which text does he take the nine parts, and is his last clause («the ninth part is the last watch; its first half is the eighth part») the etext's error?
- Question: do WB's commentaries (the regent's *G.ya' sel*, the manuals) say that WB's eight watches of the day-and-night (vol. 1 p. 135, vol. 2 p. 403) are the Hevajra and Kālacakra tradition's eight equal watches, four by day and four by night (svo:p271o)?
- Question: is the Svapnohana's «དེ་ཡིས་དེ་དུས་སྙིང་སྡུག་འཐོབ» to be read "then something dear to the heart comes" or "then heartache comes"; does any Tibetan commentary on the Svapnohana or a Sanskrit text of the bird verse (female vulture, house-bird, krauñca) decide it? (q799, unchanged: no gloss was found.)
- Question: is the night's name «ཐུན་གསུམ» in WB's list the Tibetan of the Sanskrit *triyāmā*, "three-watch", a name of the night in the Amarakośa and the Tibetan lists of synonyms?

### Searched and not found (RD314)

- BDRC full-text search (2026-11-10), no hit: «མཚན་ཐུན་བཞི», «མཚན་མོའི་ཐུན་བཞི», «ཐུན་རེ་ལ་ཆུ་ཚོད», «ཐུན་རེར་ཆུ་ཚོད», «མཚན་མོ་ཐུན་བཞིར», «ཉིན་མཚན་ཐུན་བརྒྱད» (WB's own words: no hit, although the 1996 etext holds them at vol. 2 p. 403, so the index reads that page differently and a miss proves little), «ཆུ་ཚོད་བདུན་ཕྱེད་རེ», «མཚན་མོ་ཐུན་བཞིར་བགོས», «ཐུན་རེ་ལ་ཆུ་ཚོད་བདུན་ཕྱེད», «མཚན་མོའི་ཐུན་བཞི་པ», «ཐུན་གསུམ་པ་ནམ་ཕྱེད», «ཆུ་ཚོད་བཅོ་ལྔ་ཐུན», «ཐུན་རེ་ལ་ཆུ་ཚོད་བཅོ་ལྔ», «ཐུན་ནི་ཆུ་ཚོད», «ཐུན་བཞི་པ་སྐྱ་རེངས», «ཐུན་གསུམ་ཞེས་པ་ནི་མཚན», «ཉིན་མཚན་ཐུན་བརྒྱད་དུ», «ཐུན་བརྒྱད་དུ་བགོས་པ» (7 hits, none read): none of the phrases tried gives a watch of seven and a half water-hours (the Indian *yāma*, an eighth of 60) or in hours.
- «ཐུན་བཞིར་བགོས» (11 hits) and «མཚན་ཐུན་བཞིར» (40): the hits read are ritual sessions of the day (བསྒོམ་ཐུན, retreat rules), not the night.
- A text that ties the Svapnohana's watches to any of these reckonings: none among the hits; only modern books quote the verse (RD262). The Narthang Tengyur etext (VEIE0OPIF6EAA761_I2KG212312, char. 420824) and the Dpe bsdur ma volume I1PD95859 carry the bird verse under unrelated outline titles (four Tārā sādhanas, MW2KG5015_1400–1417): the outline there is mislabelled, the lines are the Svapnohana's.
- «དེ་དུས་སྙིང་སྡུག་འཐོབ» and «སྙིང་སྡུག་འཐོབ»: seven hits, all the Svapnohana in its prints and Padma rdo rje's book; «སྙིང་སྡུག་ཐོབ» one hit (a modern English-Tibetan phrase book, not relevant), «སྙིང་སྡུག་ཐོབ་པར་འགྱུར», «སྙིང་སྡུག་པ་འཐོབ», «སྙིང་སྡུག་ཅེས་པ་ནི», «སྙིང་སྡུག་པ་ནི་བརྩེ་བ», «སྙིང་སྡུག་པ་ནི་ཡིད་དུ», «རྨི་ལམ་སྙིང་སྡུག», «སྙིང་སྡུག་རྙེད» none. Steinert's dictionaries were not cloned in this reading.
- «ཐུན་གསུམ་མ», «ཐུན་གསུམ་ལྡན» and «ཀུ་མུད་བཞད»: sessions of rites and WB's prints and manuals that repeat its list of the night's names (Bod kyi rtsis rig kun 'dus chen mo p. 246, Dag yig mkhas pa'i 'byung gnas, Rigs ldan zhal lung byis pa'i mig 'byed, Dpyod ldan snying nor); no Amarakośa or synonym list with the epithet was reached.
- The Tibetan Amarakośa (Mngon brjod 'chi med mdzod) was not located by these searches.
- Mipham's other writings were not searched by author (BDRC's search cannot be limited to one); the two hits with his name (the *Nye ba'i sras brgyad rtogs brjod*, a Mañjuśrī sādhana) use "the first watch of the night" and "three night sessions" and bound nothing; a third hit with his name (an alchemical text, «ཉིན་བཞི་མཚན་བཞི») concerns days and nights of a process, not watches.

## <a id="svo-p271q"></a>svo:p271q The words of the night's watches and snying sdug in the lexica (RD322)

*Read: etext, 2026-11-10.*

> Mahāvyutpatti (Steinert 21 and 63; Tibetan keys of the Sanskrit–Tibetan list, Sanskrit as printed):
> མེལ་ཚེ། praharaḥ
> ཆུ་ཚོད། ghaṭikā
> ཆུ་ཚོད། nāḍī
> ཐུན་དང་པོའམ་སྲོད། prathamapraharaḥ
> ཐུན་གཉིས་པའམ་འཇིང། dvitīyapraharaḥ
> ཐུན་གསུམ་པའམ་ལྔ་ཚིགས། tṛtīyapraharaḥ
> ཐུན་བཞི་པའམ་ནམ་གུང། caturthapraharaḥ
> ཐུན་ལྔ་པའམ་ཐོ་རངས། pañcamapraharaḥ
> སྐྱ་རེངས་ཤར་བ། aruṇodgatam
> ཉི་མ་འཆར་བ། sūryodayaḥ
> སྙིང་དུ་སྡུག་པ། hṛdyam
> 
> Tshig mdzod chen mo (Steinert 25, Wylie as the dictionary is keyed):
> thun gsum pa | {(mngon) mtshan mo/}
> gung thun | {nyin mo thun bzhir dbye ba'i thun gsum pa'o/}
> dgung thun | {nyin mtshan so sor thun gsum gsum phye ba'i thun bar ma'am thun gnyis pa/}
> snying du sdug pa | {yid du 'ong ba'am 'bab pa/ mi de kun gyi snying du sdug par 'dzin pa/}
> 
> Bod yig tshig gter rgya mtsho (Steinert 62):
> snying sdug | {mdza' grogs sam yid la shin tu 'bab pa'i mi pho mo/}
> mtshan thun | {nam gyi cha gsum gang rung /} {mtshan thun gsum du bgos pa'i thun tha ma la skya rengs gtong /}
> 
> Dung dkar tshig mdzod chen mo (Steinert 34):
> thun gsum | {snga dro phyi dro dgung thun gsum lta bu}
> 
> Brda dkrol gser gyi me long (Steinert 55):
> gung thun | {mtshan mo'i cha shas brgyad kyi thun gsum pa'i ming ste/} 《{dag yig mkhas pa'i 'byung gnas}》{las/ rgyu skar shar ba dang / mtshan mo'i thun dang po/ thun dang po'i phyed/ gung thun/ gung thun gyi phyed/ thun tha ma/ thun tha ma'i phyed/ skya rengs ma shar ba ste mtshan mo'i cha shas brgyad do/ / zhes gsungs/ ...
> 
> Negi, Tibetan–Sanskrit dictionary (Steinert 50):
> thun gsum | = {mtshan mo} triyāmā, rātriḥ — atha śarvarī niśā niśīthinī rātristriyāmā kṣaṇadā kṣapā vibhāvarītamasvinyau rajanī yāminī tamī a.ko.1.4. 5; trayo yāmā yasyāḥ sā triyāmā a.vi.1.4.5.
> thun gsum pa | 1. tṛtīyapraharaḥ ma.vyu.8242 2. = {mtshan mo} triyāmā, rātriḥ ṅa.ko.69/rā.ko.2.661.
> snying sdug | = {snying du sdug pa/}
> snying du sdug pa | = {snying sdug} • vi. priyaḥ … dayitaḥ … manoharaḥ … hṛdayaṃgamaḥ … hṛdyam ma.vyu.2725
> 
> Rangjung Yeshe (Steinert 2): snying sdug | a loved one {snying sdug sems la bkod pa'i zug gzer dum bu de} the fierce pangs that build up in the mind when it yearns for a loved one
> Rangjung Yeshe: snying sdug | boy friend, girl friend; syn {dga' rogs}
> Valby (Steinert 7): snying sdug | darling, fiance, beloved, beloved one, sweetheart, love, husband, friend
> Valby: thun gsum pa | night; one who meditates 3 times a day, lunar crescent on 3rd day after new or full moon, night
> Ives Waldo (Steinert 8): snying sdug | sweetheart, girl friend
> Sgra bye brag tu rtogs byed chen mo (Steinert 59): snying sdug | (Skt. hṛdyam) pleasing to the heart, dear, beloved, cherished, desired, grateful
> 
> Dung dkar (Steinert 34): thun drug | {nyin mtshan gyi thun bsdoms na thun drug yod de/skya rengs shar nas nyi ma ma shar bar nyin thun dang po/nyi ma shar nas nyin gung ma zin par nyin thun gnyis pa/nyin gung yol nas nyi ma ma nub bar nyin thun gsum pa/nyi ma nub nas srod ma yol bar mtshan thun dang po/sa srod yul nas nam gung ma shar bar mtshan thun gnyis pa/nam khung yol nas skya rengs ma shar bar mtshan thun gsum pa bcas yin}
> Tshig mdzod chen mo (Steinert 25): nyin mtshan thun drug | nyin mo'i thun gsum dang mtshan mo'i thun gsum ste drug de ni snga dro dang|{nyi gung/ phyi dro/ srod/ nam gung/ tho rangs bcas yin/}
> Tshig mdzod chen mo: mtshan thun | {nam gyi cha gsum gang rung/ mtshan thun gsum du bgos pa'i thun tha ma la skya rengs gtong/}

Read 2026-11-10 (RD322) in Steinert's public dictionaries (github.com/christiansteinert/tibetan-dictionary, sparse clone of `_input/dictionaries/public*`, commit 69969d7 of 2026-10-20), as the headwords and entries are keyed there; the dictionaries' scans were not opened, so the state is etext. Pages are those of the scan indexes in Steinert's no. 63 (Mahāvyutpatti), 65 (Chandra Das) and 66 (Jäschke), where a question mark after the number is Steinert's own.

**The Mahāvyutpatti (no. 21, Sanskrit–Tibetan order; no. 63 gives the page).** In the section of the day's times the list runs: watch (mel tshe, མེལ་ཚེ) = praharaḥ; water-hour (chu tshod, ཆུ་ཚོད) = ghaṭikā and nāḍī; first watch, or srod (thun dang po'am srod) = prathamapraharaḥ; second watch, or 'jing (thun gnyis pa'am 'jing) = dvitīyapraharaḥ; third watch, or lnga tshigs (thun gsum pa'am lnga tshigs, ཐུན་གསུམ་པའམ་ལྔ་ཚིགས) = tṛtīyapraharaḥ; fourth watch, or nam gung (thun bzhi pa'am nam gung) = caturthapraharaḥ; fifth watch, or tho rangs (thun lnga pa'am tho rangs) = pañcamapraharaḥ; then dawn breaking (skya rengs shar ba) = aruṇodgatam, sunrise (nyi ma 'char ba) = sūryodayaḥ. The watch entries stand on p. 426 of the scan index of no. 63 (the water-hour's with a question mark). The same list holds "snying du sdug pa" = hṛdyam on p. 183 and "sdug pa" = kāntaḥ on p. 182 and = praṇayaḥ on p. 478. The Mahāvyutpatti has five watches of the night in this list, the third of five being the thun gsum pa; Negi cites the same entry as ma.vyu.8242 and the heart-pleasing word as ma.vyu.2725 (the list's own numbering is not in Steinert's file).

**Negi's Tibetan–Sanskrit dictionary (no. 50, no page in the file).** "thun gsum" = (the night, mtshan mo) triyāmā, rātriḥ, with the Amarakośa's synonym verse (a.ko. 1.4.5: "atha śarvarī niśā niśīthinī rātristriyāmā kṣaṇadā kṣapā vibhāvarītamasvinyau rajanī yāminī tamī") and the gloss "trayo yāmā yasyāḥ sā triyāmā" (a.vi. 1.4.5, Amarakośa's commentary). "thun gsum pa" = 1. tṛtīyapraharaḥ (ma.vyu. 8242), 2. the night, triyāmā, rātriḥ (ṅa.ko. 69, rā.ko. 2.661). "snying sdug" and "snying sdug pa" are sent to "snying du sdug pa", which Negi gives as the adjective priyaḥ, dayitaḥ, manoharaḥ, hṛdayaṃgamaḥ, rucyam and hṛdyam (ma.vyu. 2725), and as a noun (the entry goes on).

**Tibetan lexica on thun gsum pa.** Tshig mdzod chen mo (no. 25): "thun gsum pa" is (in the synonym register, mngon) "night" (mtshan mo); "gung thun" is the third of the day divided into four; "mtshan thun": any one of the night's three parts, and "when the night is cut in three watches, the dawn is let go in the last watch". Valby (no. 7): "thun gsum pa" = night, also one who meditates three times a day, the lunar crescent on the third day. Ives–Waldo (no. 8) lists "thun gsum pa" among the night's names and "thun" as 'shift, night watch'. Chandra Das (no. 65) has "thun gsum pa" on p. 581 (?), Jäschke (no. 66) "thun gsum", "thun gsum pa", "thun gsum pa'am lnga tshigs" and "thun gsum ril bu" on p. 259 (?); the page images were not read. Dung dkar (no. 34) glosses "thun gsum" itself only as the day's three: morning, afternoon, midday. The same dictionary and Tshig mdzod chen mo give the day-and-night of six sessions: day, dawn-to-sunrise, sunrise-to-before noon, noon-to-sunset; night, sunset to before dusk passes, from dusk to before midnight rises, from midnight to before dawn: three of the day and three of the night. Brda dkrol gser gyi me long (no. 55) takes from the Dag yig mkhas pa'i 'byung gnas eight parts of the night: the rising of the travelling mansion (rgyu skar shar ba), the first watch, its half, the middle watch (gung thun), its half, the last watch, its half, and the time before dawn breaks, and gives the entry gung thun as "the name of the thun gsum pa among the night's eight parts", the watch that stands between the first and the last in that list.

**"Snying sdug".** No dictionary gives it as pain of the heart. Rangjung Yeshe (no. 2): "a loved one", with the example of the pangs of yearning for a loved one, and "boy friend, girl friend"; Valby (no. 7): "darling, fiancé, beloved, sweetheart, love, husband, friend"; Ives–Waldo (no. 8): "sweetheart, girl friend"; Bod yig tshig gter rgya mtsho (no. 62): "a friend or a person, man or woman, who strongly appeals to the mind"; Tshig mdzod chen mo (no. 25) under "snying du sdug pa": "agreeable, or appealing; one whom everyone holds dear"; the Sgra bye brag tu rtogs byed chen mo (no. 59): "(Skt. hṛdyam) pleasing to the heart, dear, beloved, cherished, desired, grateful". Chandra Das (p. 503) and Jäschke (p. 223) have the headword; their text was not read. In the same Tshig mdzod chen mo, the verb "sdug pa" also means "sorrow, to weep from sadness" (skyo ba, sems sdug nas ngu ba), a separate sense of the syllable. The medical sets of the collection (no. 35, Thomas's doctor's dictionary, and no. 48, the Tibetan Terminology Project) have no headword "snying sdug"; the 64 dictionaries give it nowhere as an illness or a sign.

> 1. Colophon in the Go rams pa gsung 'bum etext (VEIE0OPIBBE88D74_I2PD17687, etext p. 474), the end of «འདུལ་བ་མདོ་རྩ་བའི་སྤྱི་དོན་ལེགས་བཤད་འོད་ཀྱི་སྣང་བ་ཚིག་དོན་རབ་གསལ»:
> 
> འདུལ་བ་མདོ་རྩ་བའི་སྤྱིའི་རྣམ་པར་གཞག་པ་བད
> གཞན་རབ་ཕན་ཚིག་དོན་རབ་གསལ་ཞེས་བྱ་བ་འདི་ནི། ཐོག་མར་དཔལ་ལྡན་ས་སྐྱ་པའི་གདུང་
> བརྒྱུད་དྲི་མ་མེད་པར་སྐུ་འཁྲུངས་ཤིང་། །མཁྱེན་རབས་དང་ཐུགས་རྗེ་ཆེད་མེད་པ་དང་ལྡན་པ།
> སློབ་དཔོན་ཆེན་པོ་ངག་གི་དབང་ཕྱུག་གྲགས་པ་རྒྱ་མཚན་དཔལ་བཟང་པོའི་བཀས་བསྐུལ་ཞིང༌།
> གཞན་ཡང་བློ་གསལ་ཞིང་ཡིད་གཞུངས་པའི་སྡེ་སྣོད་འཛིན་པ་དུ་མས་ཡང་ཡང་བསྐུལ་བའི་ངོར།
> ཤར་ཀོང་པོའི་ཆོས་སྨྲ་བའི་དགེ་སློང་དབང་ཕྱུག་གྲུབ་པ་དཔལ་བཟང་པོས། དངོས་པོ་ཞེས་བྱ་བ་
> ཤིང་ཕོ་ཁྱིའི་ལོ། ཧོར་ཟླ་བཅུ་གཅིག་པ། མགོ་ཡིས་ཉ་བའི་ཡར་ཚེས་ལྔ་ལ་ཐུབ་བསྟན་རྣམ་པར་
> རྒྱལ་བའི་ཆོས་གྲྭ་ཆེན་པོར་ལེགས་པར་གྲུབ་པའི་ཡི་གེ་པ་ནི། ལྷན་སྐྱེས་ཀྱི་བློ་གྲོས་མི་དམན་པ་
> དཀོན་མཆོག་དཔལ་གྱིས་བགྱིས་སོ། །
> 
> (The etext's first lines are damaged: «བད / གཞན་རབ་ཕན» for the rest of the title; the year line is as printed.)
> 
> 2. The editors' preface to the Sakya Gsung rab volume 3 (VEIE0OPIFD211FF5_I2PD18409, etext p. 11):
